Act. Jhe present dispute has been raised after a lapse ot 
about 6 years and as such the reference is bad. It is sub¬ 
mitted on behalf of the management that the demand ol 
the workmen tor ic-employment is not justified ami that 
they are not entitled to any relief. 

The question to be determined in this reference is whether 
the demand tor re-employment ot me concerned workmen 
is justified. 

None of the parties adduced any oral evidence. How¬ 
ever, tne management has exhibited tnrcc bonus legisters m 
borm X. which are marked Ext. M-l, M-2 and tvr-3. The 
workmen did not exhibit any uoctiment, 

Some facts in tho case are admitted. Jt is admitted that 
Sint. Liunta Kamin, and Smt, Gogi Namin were working as 
wagon loaders in Govindpur Colliery of the management and 
that Smt. Chtnia ivannn had absented from duty w-ith dlect 
irom 15-12-77 and Smt. Gogi Kaniin had absented from 

23- 12-77 without any permission. It is admitted that the 
concerned workmen Shrj Jitan Bhuia was working as at) 
Underground trammer and absented from duty from 18-3-7/ 
and Shri Budhan Otaon was working as a Driller and absented 
from duty from 2-5-78 without any permission. It is also 
admitted that the names of the concerned workmen were 
removed from the rolls by the management on the grotiud 
mat they had abandoned their employment. There is dispute 
between the parties on the lact whether the concerned work¬ 
men were casual workmen or were permanent workmen and 
whether they wete in continuous service for a period of one 
year and that they had put iu more than 240 days attendance 
within 12 calendar inonlhs prior to their removal from the 
service, I will take up first this disputed question of fact. 

JP.xt. M-2 is the Bonus Register in Form X for the year 
1977. It will appear from this register at page-145 that 
Smt. Chinta Kamin had worked for 107 days from first 
quarter of January, 1977 to the fourth quarter of January, 
1977. It is admitted case of the parties that she had absented 
from duty with effect from 15-12-77 and as such the period 
of 12 calendar months ha s to be calculated from 15-12-76. 
Even il wc take that Smt. Chinta Kamin was present for all 
the 16 days from 15-12-76 to 31-12-76, even then the total 
attendance of Smt, Chinta Kamin would not come to 240 
days in the preceeding 12 calendar months. It will further' 
appear from Ext. M-2 at page-146 that Smt. Gogi Kamin had 
worked for 97 days from ihe first quarter of 1977 to the 
fourth quarter of 1977. Admittedly, she absented from 

24- 12-77. In order to find out whether Smt. Gogi Kamin 
had .worked 240 days in the 12 preceeding months the 
calculation has to be made from 24-12-76 and even if it be 
taken that she was present on all the days in the month of 
December, 1976 her attendance will fall far short of 240 
days within the preceeding 12 calendar months prior to her 
removal from service. The said attendance noted down in 
Ext. M-2 has not been challenged on behalf of the concerned 
workmen and I see no reason to disbelieve the attendance 
of the above two concerned workmen noted in Ext. M-2. 


it is clear, therefore, that the two concerned workmen Smt. 
khiniU Kanjm and Smt. Gogi Kamin had, not completed 
24U days aucndauce tn a year prior to their temoval lrom 
ihe roll. 

Ext. M-l is the Bonus Register for the year i978. At 
page ul or me said register u will appear that lire concerned 
workman Budlun Oraon had 41 days oL" attendance during 
Hie iiist quarter and second quarter of 1978. Admittedly, 
budnan oraon had absented irom duty front 2-5-78. this 
tcgi-ccr E\l. M-l is therefore, only in respect ol lour months 
ui tne year 1978 and it appears that tilt i-5-78 Budhan Oraon 
had 4i days of attendance at his eicdit. The management 
did not nte the Bonus Register for the year 1977 in order 
to show as to what was the attendance of Budhan Oraon in 
the previous 7 months ol the year 1977. No explanation 
has been given as to why the management has not produced 
the Bonus Register of 1977 in respect of Budhan Oraon and 
it appears obvious that the production of the bonus Register 
of 1977 would have shown his attendance over 240 days in 
tne 12 calendar months prior to the removal of the nunie of 
Budhan Oraon from the rolls. In my opinion this register 
E/d. M-l cannot be used by the management to show that 
Budhan Oraon had only 41 days of attendance in the 12 
preceding months prior to his removal from the service. 
It is stated in the VV.S. of the concerned workmen that he 
was a permanent driller and I his fact has not been denied 
on behalf of the management. It is clear, therefore, that 
Budhan Oraon was a permanent workman. 

Ext. M-3 i s the Bonus Register for the year 1977, It will 
appear from P-121 of this register that one Jitan Bhuia 
had put in attendance for 229 days Irom the lirst quarter of 
1977 to Ihe fourth quarter of 1977. The admitted case of 
the paitieb is that the concerned workmen Jitan Bhuia had 
absented from duty from 18-3-77 and as such the attendance 
of Jitan Bhuia shuwn in Ext. M-3 after 18-3-77 in this register 
is either in respect of some other Jitan Bhuia or the entries 
arc false. On caieful perusal of page 121 it appears that, 
probably this Jitan Bhuia was some different Jitan Bhuia 
because originally his father’s name was noted as Puna Bhuia 
which vvas penued through and the father’s name was noted 
as Ualbhadar Bhuia. In his case also us in the ease of 
Budhan Oraon, the Bonus Register for the year 1976 has 
not been produced to show his attendance as Ihe period of 
12 months is to be calculated from the month of March, 
J97n. The management has not explained as to wbv tho 
Bonus Register for the year 1976 in respect of Jitan Bhuia 
has no! been produced. The non-production of either atten¬ 
dance register or Bonus Register will show that the manage¬ 
ment l .:s not been able to establish that Jilan Bhuia had not 
woiked for over 240 days within the 12 months prior to 
the removal of his name from the roll specially when Jitan 
Bhuia was a permanent workman. 

On consideration of Ihe Bunus Registers, filed on behalf 
of the management it appears that the management has not 
produced document to establish that the concerned work¬ 
men Budhan Oraon and .Titan Bbuiu had put in less than 
240 days of attendance in the last 12 calendar months prior 
to ihe icmovat of (heir names front the rolls although the 
records regarding attendance of (he workmeu are within 
the possession of the management. 

From the discussion of the facts made above the case of 
the four concerned workmen can be classified in two groups. 
On e group consists of two days .workmen who do not appear 
to have Completed 240 days of attendance in the preceeding 
12 months prior to the removal of their names from the 
roll. The second group is in relation the remaining, two 
concerned workmen, namely, Jitan Bhuia and Budhan Oraon 
who are permanent workmen working since long and iri 
whose respect it has not been established that thev had 
worked for less than 240 days in the last preceding 12 
months prior to the removal of their names from the rolk 
1 would first, take up the group of Ihe two lady workmen 
Sml. Chinta Kamin and Smt. Gogi Kamin. Admittedly, 
they have not been removed from service for any misconduct. 
Jr is staled in para 9 of Ihe Wi.S. of the management that 
since they continuously absented for sufficiently long period, 
il was deemed that they had abandoned their respective em¬ 
ployment and therefore their names were removed from tb? 
rolls. Thus it is the clear case of the management that 
they have treated the absence of these two lady workmen 
as abandonment of their job bv their own act of absence. 
According to the concerned workmen their case is that the 
removal of their names from the niustei; roll is retrenchment 



-Div 


[.Pari 11—sec. 3(uj] 


1JNUJA ■ U-L-• BfiK it* , l. u AUSA 8, lyUb 


and as the name has been removed from the muster roll 
without paying any retrenchment compensation, the action 
of the. management in removing them from service was illegal 
and not justified, in order to avail of the provisions of 
Section 25-F of the I. E>. Act the concerned workmen have to 
show that they were in continuous service for not less tfjan 
one year under the management. Continuous service has been 
defined in Section 23-B of the I. D. Act. It will appear from 
the Bonus Register Ext. M-2 that the date of appointment of 
Gogi Ramin is shown as 6-3-72 and that of Chinta Ramin a» 
11-1-73. There does not appear to be any evidence that they 
had completed 240 days of attendance in the last 12 calendar 
months prior to the removal of the names from the rolls of 
the workmen. These two lady concerned workmen, on the 
tacts of case as discussed above, do not appear to have 
attendance for 240 days under the management during the 
period of 12 calendar months preceding the date with refe¬ 
rence to which calculation has to be made. In the above 
view of the matter I am of the opinion that the two lady 
concerned workmen arc not entitled to the benefit of the 
provision of the Section 25-F of the I. D. Act. I hold that 
the removal of the names of two lady concerned workmen 
from the roll of the management cannot be construed as 
retrenchment by the management and as they were casual 
workmen having less than 240 days of attendance during the 
period 12 calendar months preceding the date of their 
removal from service, they cannot claim any retrenchment 
benefit or advantage of re-employment on the ground that 
the removal of their names from the roll should be construed 
not as abandonment but as retrenchment. In the above 
view of the matter the demand lor the rc-cniployment of 
Smt. Chinta Ramin and Smt. Gogi Ramin is not justified. 

Now we take up the case of the other group consisting 
of Jitan Bhuia and Budhan Oraon. Once again I would 
repeat and refer to para 9 of the W.S. of the management 
wherein they have stated that the workmen had not worked 
for the requisite number of days during the preceeding 12 
months ana that since they continuously absented for suffi¬ 
ciently long period it was deemed that Ibcy had abandoned 
then- respective employment and therefore their names were 
removed from the rolls of the workmen. The case of these 
two concerned workmen is quite distinct on facts from the 
case of the two lady concerned workmen whose case I have 
already discussed above. It has already been discussed and 
found above that it has-not been established that Jitan Bhuia 
and Budhan Oraon had less than 240 days of attendance 
during the 12 calendar months preceeding the date of the 
removal of their names from the roll and that they were 
admittedly permanent workmen of the management and us 
such I hold that they had.completed 240 days of attendance 
during the 12 months preceeding removal of their names 
from the rolls. The management have treated their absence 
as abandonment. It is stated on behalf of the management 
that the concerned workmen had absented because of illness. 

It is further stated in para 9 of the rejoinder of the con¬ 
cerned workmen in reply to the W.S. or the workmen that 
it is absolutely false to allege that the concerned workmen 
abandoned their jobs as after sometime the concerned work¬ 
men had reported for their duty but were not allowed to 
resume their duties. This assertion lhat the concerned 
workmen had reported for duty but were not allowed to 
resume has not been controverted in any rejoinder filed on 
behalf of the management. Thus the fact remains un¬ 
challenged that these two concerned workmen had reported 
for their duty after sometime of their absence and that they 
were not allowed to resume their duties. If the concerned 
workmen had reported for their duties it was obvious that 
they had no intention of abandoning their job. The learned 
representative of the workmen has cited SCU-Vol-15-P-l 
where the meaning of abondonment of service has been dealt 
with. Their Lordships have stated that ‘‘abondonment must 
be total and under such circumstances as clearly to indicate 
an absolute relinquishment. The failure to perform the 
duties pertaining to the office must be with actual or imputed 
Intention, on the part of the officer to abondon and relinquish 
the office. The intention may be inferred from the acts and 
conduct of the party, and is a question of fact. Temporary 
absence is not ordinarily sufficient to constitute an abandon¬ 
ment of office. To constilute abandonment there must be total 
or complete giving up of duties so as to indicate an intention 
not to resume the same. Thus, whether there has been a 
voluntary abandonment of service or not is a question of 
fact which has to be determined in the light of the surround¬ 
ing circumstances of each case.” It will appear in the 
present case from the W.S. of the concerned workmen that 
after they had absented for sometime, they had reported for 


duty but it was refused by the management. Thus it is 
cicur that the concerned workmen nau no intention m 
abandonment of iheir services and that they had actually 
reported for duty atrer sometime. U is clear, therefore that 
the absence of me two concerned workmen qpnnol be taken 
as abandonment, 

It has been submitted on behalf of tne concerned work¬ 
men trial the removal of then' names from the roll by the 
management cannot be taken as abandonment but is a case 
which is covered under Section 25-F ol tile i. D. Act. In 
support ol iheir case a latest decision reported in 1984 Lab. 
J.L. F-16M may usefully be referred, lln said dcision is 
a Division Bench case of the Hon’ble fiign Court of Judi¬ 
cature at Patna. It has been held "Whether the termination 
of service is brought about by voluntary or involuntary action 
whether that result is pioduccd bv over tact or by operation 
of the provisions of Standing Order the termination would be 
retrenchment within the meaning of S. 2(oo) since the fact 
of termination is only relevant, howsoever produced is rele¬ 
vant for the applicability of S. 25-F. Once ihe termination 
does not fall in any one of the excepted categories enumera¬ 
ted in S. 2(oo) ,ihe termination of sorviee even if it be 
according to automatic discharge trom service under an 
agreement or by efflux of time or by the default of the 
workman it would be retrenchment attracting the compliance 
of S. 25-F fa) and tb) of the Act.' 1 

It is further held in the said decision as follows ;—■ 

“Since termination of service amounting tp retrench¬ 
ment without compliance with the provisions of 
S, 25-F renders the termination void ab initio and 
inoperative, it cannot be said that in such a case 
the employee would not be entitled to reinstate¬ 
ment and back wages.” 

The facts of the case referred to above is that tile petitioner 
was employed as Burner in Rohtas Industries in the year 
1949. Because of his ill health, he first went on sick leave 
which he extended and thereafter he was absenting without 
leave. As the petitioner remained absent beyond the period 
of leave originally granted and subsequently extended and 
did not return to his duties within 10 days of the expiry of 
the leave, he had lost his lien on his appointment under 
the Standing Orders of the Company. The facts of the said 
case are almost similar to our ease in this reference and as 
such there is absolutely no distinguishing feature as to why 
the principles applied in 1984 Lab. 1.C.-1651, which is based 
on the principles decided by the Hon’ble Supreme Court, 
should not apply with all forms in the presence reference. 

I hold, therefore, that the removal ot the names of the 
concerned workmen Shri Jitan Bhuia and Budhan Oraon 
from the- rolls cannot be treated as abandonment and that 
tho said termination of service will be retrenchment attract¬ 
ing the compliance of Section 25-F (a)tb) of the I. D. 
Act, 

Admittedly, the case of these two concerned workmen 
has not been treated as rcttenchment and no retrenchment 
compensation etc. have been paid and Ihe provisions of 
Section 25-F have not been complied with. Since the termi¬ 
nation of the services amounts to retrenchment without com¬ 
pliance with the provisions of the Section 25-F of the I. D. 
Act, the termination of their service is void ab initio and 
in-operative. There is no question of granting reinstate¬ 
ment because there is no cessation of service. As is held in 
1984 Lab. J. C. P.-1651 a mere declaration that the employer 
continues to be on service with all consequential benefits 
is enough Hnd accordingly 1 hold that the concerned work¬ 
men Jitan Bhuia and Budhan Oraon continue to be in service 
with all consequential benefits from the date from which 
their names were removed from the rolls. 

Jn view of the discussions made above I hold that the 
demand of the workmen of Govindpur Colliery of M/s. 
Bharat Coking Coal Ltd. for re-employment of Smt. Chinta 
Ramin and Smt. Gogi Ramin is not justified and as such 
these two lady concerned workmen are entitled to no relief. 

I further hold that the demand of the workmen of Govind- 
pur Colliery of M/s. Bharat Coking Coal T td for re-em¬ 
ployment of the concerned workmen S/Shri Jitan Bhuia and 
Budhan Oraon by ihe management is justified. The above 
two concerned workmen, namely, Jitan Bhuia and Budhan 
Oraon shall he entitled to continue in service wfith all benefits 


[vnu j(ii)] mn *ft wh : fw 

hnm the date (fair nam.'s were inriovw] from the ioIIp of 
the management. 

This is my Award. 

I. N. SINHA, Presiding Officer

2. The reference was received in this Tribunal on 27-9-84.

taken on behalf of the union. 

3. Tt is for the sponsoring union to file the statement of 

claim, etc. within 15 days of the receipt of the order of 

reference. But the Tribunal took special care to give them 

notice to comply the provisions of law in time aR the 

amended rules are new. As the union is taking no step and 
has not even cared lo file the statement of claim etc. it 
appears that they have no case and as such, the statement 
of claim, etc. have not been filed by them

1982.

“Whether action of the management of Gidi Washery

The case of the management is that on 23-2-81 at about

worekrs who were given opportunity to cross-examine the

management's witnesses. The workmen and his co-workers

Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government 
hereby publishes the following award of the Central Govern¬ 
ment Industrial Tribunal, New Delhi in the industrial dispute 
between the employers in relation to the management of 
Food Corporation of India and their workman which was 
received by the Central Government on tho 31st November, 
1984. ...'MiiiWM ■ 

BEFORE SHRI O P. SINGLA, PRESIDING OFFICER, 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT, INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL, 
NEW DELHI 

I. D. No. 176/83 

In the matter of dispute between : 

Mohd. Asif Hashmi S/o Shri Ayaub Ali R/o 14-J 
Extension, Part II, Laxmi Nagar, Delhi-92. 

Versus 

The Managing Director, Food Corporation of India, 
16—20, Barakhamba Lane, New Delhi, 

APPEARANCES : 

Shri K. A. Dewan—for the E. C. I. 

Shri K. S. Sahal —for the workman. 

AWARD 

Central Government, Ministry of Labour on 11th April, 
1983 vide Order No. L-42012/37/82-D.1I (B) made reference 
of the following dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication : 

“Whether the action of the management of Food Cor¬ 
poration of India, New Delhi in reverting Shri 
Mohd. Asif Hashmi w.e.f. 15-8-1980 from the post 
of typist to the post of peon is justified ? If not, 
to whut relief the workman is entitled ?” 

2. The dispute was espoused by the - Bhartiya Khadya 
Nigam Kuramchari Sangh, The workman joined the F.C.I. 

a watchman on 27-6-73 and was promoted as typist w.e.f. 
19-9-78 vide office order No. 6(14)/78-E. II dated 19-9-1978. 
He claims that he was placed on probation for a period of 
one year and became entitled to confirmation after one year. 
He asserted that on the basis of manipulated annual jeport 
prepared subsequently he was reverted as Peon by office 
order No. 10/13/78-E. II dated 15-9-80 much after the 
expiry of one year probation. He claimed that tho continued 
to be a typist and the orders of reversion were mala fide, 
wrong and illegal. 

3. The Food Corporation of India Management contested 
the claim and pleaded that this case of single workman could 
not be referred by the Central Government. On merits it 
was pleaded that the action of reversion was perfactly justi¬ 
fied within the framework of Ffl Stuff Regulations 1971. 
During the period of probation as typist his work was not 
satisfactory and the Appointing Authority did not consider 
him fit for confirmation. He should be reverted without 
aligning any reason and probation could not be 
eomoleted satisfactorily till confirmation by the Competent 
Authority within two years of tho date of promotion. He 
was reverted within two years of his probation. 

4. The matter has been tried. The relevant rule in this 
resnect has been quoted by the management in para 15 as 
tinder :— 

“15. Probation 

(J) Every person regularly anpointed to any post in 
the Corporation under Sub-clause (a) of Clause, 
(1) of Regulation 7 shall be required to be on 
probation for a period of one year from the date 
of appointment. 



WTltf tNr<T5T.'fafTOH: 29, 19B i/'M 8, 1906 


4519 


(ii)l 


(2) The appointment authority may in his discretion 
extend the period of probation by a further period 
not exceeding one year. 

(3) During the period of probation an employee directly 
recanted shall be liable to be discharged from 
service without assigning any reason by giving him 
a notice of 30 day6 or pay in lieu thereof.” 

5. 15(2) aforesaid allows probation to be extended by a 
period of one year but in this particular case there was no 
actual extension of probation and the ordinary one year 
period probation expired in September, 79 without’ there 
being any order of reversion. 

6. Under the circumstances the workman is on a sure 
footing when he claims that hi could not be reverted and 
even fits evidence comes out that he has been functioning 
as typist continuously since 19-9-78. 

7. The. Management’s concession to set aside the order 
of reversion on workman agreeing to fresh appointment 
as typist is there but the workman need not be put to that 
election because tire order of reversion order in post of typist 
is itself illegal and unjustified in the circumstances of 
this case. Accordingly this tribunal rules that the reversion 
of the workman Mohd. Asif Hasmi w.e.f. 19-9-80 
from the post of typist to the post of Peon was unjustified 
and he is entitled to be treated as confirmed w.e.f. 19-9-80 
and to consequential benefits on that basis. The Award i6 
made accordingly. 

Further it is ordered that the requisite number of copies 
of this Award may be forwarded to the Central Government 
for necessary action at their end. 

Dated : November 27, 1984. 

O. P. SING LA, Presiding Officer

On a perusal of the above issue it become* clear that the 
demand relates only to proper fixation of pay. The written 
statement of the union is not very clear and specific because 
it has not been said specifically In any of the paragraph or 
the written statement as to in what manner and what amount 
are being claimed by them. Although the union has an¬ 
nexed some annexures to their written statement but those 
annexures have not been explained in the written statement 
which ought to have been done. Any way the demand of 
the onion is understandable from the evidence of WW-1, 
Asit Boran B&roa, which I shall deal later. The case of 
the management of the ECL, Sanctoria, P.O. Disargarh is that 
the pay of the concerned workmen has already been pro¬ 
perly fixed under NCWA-TI and the demand has been made 
on baseless grounds. 

2. I wiif now deal with the justification of the demand 
which is! entirely based on the provisions of clause 4.3.2 of 
NCWA-II. That clause is as follows : 

“4.3,2 The dates from which annual increments will be 
drawn in the revised scales will be ; 

Those whose increments fair due between 1-1-79 to 
28-2-79 1-3-1979. 

Those whose increments fall due between 1-3-79 and 
31-8-79 .1-9-1979. 

These whose increments fall the between 1-9 79 and 
28-2-80 -1 3-1980. 

The amounts already drawn in the existing scales under 
NCWA-I, including the increments, would be ad¬ 
justed from the amounts that become due in terms 



4520 TllE GAZETTE OF INDIA : DECEMBeA 29, 1984/PA US A-tf, 1906 IPart II— Sic. 3(ii)] 


oi the revised wages, including, the increments spelt 
out in this para. 


ti' is clear tbijt the third pait of the clause which applies 
ty the instant case is "those whose lntienients fall due bet¬ 
ween 1-9-79 and 28 2-tlll ....1-3-19811’'. it may he noted 
that the date 1-3-1980 is the date from which the annual 
increment will be drawn in the revised scales under NOV.r- 
11. In the present case WW-1, Asit Harun llama has ad 
iiiillfd in his evidence that his increment fell due to Sep¬ 
tember 1979. His evidence is that normally they were getting 
incicmen.-s in the month of September every year. He ad- 
mined that the concerned workmen received the increment 
in the munth of September 1978 upto 31st December 1978.' 
In the cioss-ejtamintition he said that the annual increments 
(n respect of the concerned workmen fell due in 14th Sep¬ 
tember 1979. Obviously, tlierefoie, the case ol the con¬ 
cerned workmen falls under the third part aforesaid and 
hence the date from which the annual increment will be 
dr, v,n b\ them in the revised scale will be 1 March 1980. 
in his evidence \VW 1, A. H. Btirua has clearly said that 
normally they were getting increment in .September of every 
5’etlr and that they have not received the 'inclement in the 
year. 1979 for which.they were seeking justice. It is in¬ 
creasingly clear that the mitih grievance relates to the pay- 
n*.nt of Ihc increment which fell due in September 1979. 
But from the aforesaid clause 4.3.2 G NCWA-Il dated 
11th August 1979 which came into force with effect from 
1-1-1979 il is clear that in such a case they will be entitled 
to the inctemcnt in revised scules only from 1-3 1950. That 
has been paid. There is no dispute with regaul to 'this 
fact. The grievance of the union is thus bnsed on a wrong 
reading of clause 4.3.2 of NCWA-lI. After NCWA-Il 
enme into force it will be the revised scales cf pay which 
will lie applicable to the concerned workmen and not the 
old scale. The claim of the union is therefore unfounded. 
The management of the ECL in their written statement 
dated 1-2-1984 has made the position clear by giving an 
example of P. C. Rov in paragraph K of their written state-' 
ment. It is «aid that P. C. Roy was in technical ‘8’ with 
his basic salary' on January 1978 as Rs. 645 per me nth; that 
iiElet- adding, his annual increment of Rs. 27 which fell due 
on 14 September 1978 his basic changed to Rs. 672 per 
month and this was the basic salary in the month of 1 Decem¬ 
ber 1978: that as per the chart of con vets ion the basic 
salary falling: due in January 1979 becnn^ Rs. 815 per 
month under NCWA-lf effective from 1-1-1979 und as per 
provision of the aforesaid clause 4.3.2, his nett 
annual increment fell due on 1-3-1980 become Rs. 950 per 
month. The management has shown the detailed calcula¬ 
tion in respect of the concerned workmen in nnnexure 1. 
K is not necessary to go into details about every workman. 

1 have RI ready pointed out that the annual increment of the 
Concerned workmen of this reference prior to | January 
1979 fell due on 14th September 1978 and that their next 
annual increment was due to fall on 14th September 1979 
and in that view of the mutter they became entitled to 
draw their annual increment under the revued scales only 
from 1 March 1980 and not before. T have already said 
that the said annual increment was paid. The result was 
that the pay of the concerned workmen were properly fixed 
and (here is no substance in their derrond. A number of 

documents has been filed by the management and some 

documents hv the union but they do not throw anv further 

titdir on the- matter than I have already said. Only one 

thlna. needs (o be said heie, it was submitted on behalf of 
the union that there were some anomalies in the pay of the 
workmen of the senior grade in the sense that the juniors 
ware gettimr more than what the senior got at head quaiter. 
However, the said miomnlies has been removed bv the 
. manverrent bv issue of the circular ("Ext M-21 through 
JP.ftrr bv dv'ng one increment to the senior world"m. In 
his cross-examination WW-1. Asit Baran Rama admi'ted that 
uri anomaly exists in their central workshop. 


3. In the result my concluded Award is that the demand 
of the workmen listed before working in the Neam->tmir 
Centra 1 Workshop of Messrs Eastern Coalfields leaved, 
Spr-t'-r|;i p.O. Diserpnrh, District Burdwan for ’’re-" r fixa¬ 
tion of Ray according to NCWA-Il is pot justified, It 


•follows, that the concerned workmen are not entitled to any

2. Buef facts of the ease, accord-ng to the petitioner 
workman, ate that he haa been intermittently serving the 
Respondent Bank, at Us various branches, as a member of 
the subordinate staff from 7-4-1970 to l'J-8- 73; although 

the:c were a number of breaks in service yet he had put in 
more ‘.nan 240 days of it in 12 calender months within the 
meaning of Section, 25-B. It was aversd that during his 
such tcmpoiary tenure, the Respondent Bank, held a written 
Lest and atso Interviewed the qualifying candidates lor filling 
up a number of permanent vacancies in the Subordinate Stall, 
Ihe petitioner also took the said Test and on passing through 
the written examination, appeared before the Interview Board 
which, on final selection, placed him at Sr. No, 51 of the 
Select List drawn in the year 1973, obviously the appoint¬ 
ments weie to be given in accordance to the serial-wise place¬ 
ments of the selected candidates in the said List. Complaining 
against his retrenhment dated 10-8-1973 the petitioner lamen¬ 
ted mat he was not offered any appointment whatsoever, 
rather, the Management by-passed the Select-list for no 
justifiable reason and gave employment to some of his juniors 
as also to a few persons whose names did not even figures 
in the said list. 


knock him out ou the mere technicalities of law, otherwise 
when one goes for a combined reading of sections 2(k) and 
c(sj ("industrial dispute" and “workman') of the Act one 
can not possibly resist the conclusion that the Appropriate 
Government acted wisely within the ambit of its powers 
to seek adjudication of the petitioner's claim. Afier all, 
according to the common case of the parties he was in the 
employment of the Respondent Bank even at ihe time of 
appearing in the wirttep test and interview held by them 
for selection of the Subordinate stalf. As a matter of fact, 
this facility was allowed to all the eligible candidates and 
it was on merit alone that the petitioner could come up on 
the Select list; similarly there is no denying the fact that 
sf>mc of his juniors, and even outsiders, were given appoint¬ 
ment by the Management, shortly after his disengagement. 
It, therefore, involved a bona fide question of fact and law 
as to whether the Management was justified in over-looking 
a seemingly legitimate grouse of one of their own erstwhile 
worker in denying him the opportunity of employment ? In 
my considered opinion the proposition requires to be ans¬ 
wered against the Management and, as such, I decide the 
issue in favour of the petitioner-workman. 


3. Feeling hurt, the petitioner approached his Union who 
represented his case for the appropriate relief and on finding 
the Management unresponsive, raised an industrial dispute 
which, however, defied any amicable settlement despite the 
intervention of the Conciliation Officer, hence the Refernce. 

4. Retiming the proceedings, the Management challenged 
lbe validity oi the Reference on the pica that the petitioner’s 
claim, which tantamounted to seeking of employment on the 
saengih or a Select List, could not be equated with any 
contentions issue relevant to the terms of employment so as 
to be covered by the definition of an "industrial dispute." 
It wus suomilted that since his tenure of employment came 
to an eno with the afflux of time, therefore, it could not be 
agitated befoie the Tribunal. On facts it was not denied 
that lie had served the Bank for quite some time in tempo¬ 
rary capaicty and had also qualified the written teat and 
interview lot being absorbed in the Subordinate Staff in 
response to an Advertisement issued hy them, and ultima¬ 
tely pljced at Sr. No. 51 of the merit list. But it was ex¬ 
plained that by the time they could absorb only 23 incum¬ 
bent* the list had to be abrogated altogether under the ins¬

opportunity of employment to the members of the Scheduled

dependants besides those of the temporary Workmen who 
had pul in 240 days of service in 12 calender months; more¬ 
over the backlog of the Reserved quota had also lo be 
filled up. 

5. Elaborating their case, the Management conceded that 
5 persons placed below the petitioner in the Select-list and 

. 4 others whose names did not figure in that List were given 
appointments but justification was projected on the plea

taker, on companionate grounds for being the heirs of the 
Bank employees dying in harness, and that at least one of 
them v as already in Bank service who had resigned his 
post from another Branch to take appointment as the junior 
most at the Branch of his choice in tile terms of Transfer 
policy prevalent at that time. For the obvious reasons any 
violation of the provisions of the Act, Bipartite Settlement 
or Awards etc. was vehemently denied.

2. Having regard lo the nature Of the dispute everything

seivices of the employee concerned was alleged to have been 
iciminaled be had put in 240 days continuous service during

case it attracts the provisions of section 25F of the Industrial

piovisions would he bud and illegal entiting the woiEmail 
to the relief or reliefs, but not otherwise. 

3. By bia written statement filed by the workman h; con¬

fore the termination being in contravention of the provisions

therefore bad. 

4. The Bank has countered all these allegations by their

never sponsored bv the Employment Exchange, his appli¬ 
cation for employment could not have been considered and 
therefore he could not have been provided for. It is further 
urged that the employee was? employed purely on casual 
basis and that he never worked for a year in any of the 
years and as such the termination even if there be anv can 
never be said to be retrenchment invoking the provisions 
of Section 25F much less Section 25B of the Industrial Dis¬ 
putes Act.

AWARD 

(Dictated in the open Court) 

By their Order No. L-13012(i)/83-D.ll (B) dated 
30-7-1984 the following dispute has been referred for ad¬ 
judication under section 10(l)fd) of the Industrial Disputes 
Act. 1947; on receipt of the failure of Conciliation Report 
im ,11 the Conciliation Officer ;— 

‘'Whether the action of the management of Cantonment 
Board, Pune, in terminating the services of Shri 

S. G. Yadav, Junior Clerk w.e.L 24-8-1979 is 
justified ? If not, to what' relief is the workman 
■ entitled ?” 

2. j\ s ;he dispute stands it relates to the services of Sbti 
S. G. Yadav a junior Clerk, who it seems served the Can- 
tonrn.'iu Board for 170 days in the year 1974, 320 days in 
the year 1975, 105 days in 1976, 187 days in 1977 and 
235 days in the year 1978. However, the record further 
indicate'^ that Shri Yadav was confirmed as a Junior Clerk 
in ilu.pital wi;h effect from 1-3-1973 but unfortunately it 
semw 011 24-8-1979 his service stood terminated as a result 
of which the present dispute has arisen. 

1 Tfi c Union who is contesting the dispute on behalf of 

lyrvknu n filed statement of claim whereby they insisted 
upon the reinstatement of the workman with back wages, 




4526 l'Hd GAZii l fu Or’ lNL)f\ : DUO'iMBRR 29, 1984/PAIT5A 8, 1906 


[Part f[—Stc. 3(ii)j| 


contended that the termination in view ol Sections 25B and 
231' of the industrial Disputes Act was illegal and therefore 
the workman is entitled to be reinstated. 

4. The Cantonment Board contested the mailer and raised 
various contentions including Lhe alleged illegality ot initial 
appointment of Shgi S. G. Vadav on the giound that his 
name was never sponsored by the Employment Exchange 
as ;t icsidt of which the Board could never hare appointed 
hint as a clerk and as such even if he had pul in ceriain 
service that service cannot be counted and no lelief is 
permissible. 

5. On the above pleadings issues were framed. 

h. However, there were negotiations between the Union 
and the Cantonment Board as u result of which the matter 
was referred to Shri A. N. Khtirpc, Member, Cantonment 
Board, Pune, who has issued office note, by which note the 
Cantonment Board is willing to abide, ohri Khurpe says 
that the workman should be reinstated in service but without 
back wages. Now since the workman was working with the 
Board utJeast from 1-2-1979 when he was coniirmed, lor 
the purpose of continuity of service that date shall have 
to be treated ns material and accordingly the workman 
must get relief. Shri Gaikwad on behalf of the work mu 
concedes that no hack wages need be paid to Shri S. G. 
Yadav from 24 8-1979 since lie did not work with the Board. 
He however, insists that though no back wages need he 
paid for the relevant period till the date of rejoining that 
period should be treated for the purpose of continuity of 
service, for which there is no objection on the pari of the 
Board from Shri Rnmakrishnan who is representing the 
said Body. The net result thereof therefore would be that 
Shri Yaciev shall he dcemecTTif be in continuous service ftoni 
1-2-1979. The period from 24-8-1979 till lhe date of re¬ 
joining shall be treated’ as extraordinary leave without pay 
and no wages or arrears shall be payable for the said period. 
However the relevant period shall be counted for the purpose 
of continuity of service and suitable benefits should flow 
from the same. Shri Yadav shall be reinstated in service 
within one month after the date of puolicition of the Award 
In the official Gazette on the same pay which he was. drawing 
on 24-8-1979 with such modification and addition which 
came info effect subsequently. However, no arrears need 
be paid to- the period before the date of rejoining. This 
period however, be treated as continuous service for the 
nurposc of pension, retirement benefits and other service 
benefits txeept for arrears of pay. Award accordingly. 

M. A. DESHPANDE, Presiding Officer

REFERENCE : 

Order No. L-42011 (8)/83-D. IHB), dated January, 1984, 
Ministry of Labour Government of India, New Delhi, 

This dispute coming on this day for final hearing upon 
perusing the reference, claim and counter statements and all 
other material papers on record and upon hearing of Thiru 
F, Rajendra. Advocate appearing for the workmen and of 
Thin* G. Rajan, Central Government leader appearing for 
the Management and the counsel for the workmen having 
made an endorsement and recording the same, this Tribunal 
passed the following, 

AWARD 

The Government of India, Ministry of Labour by it# 
Order No. L-42011 (8)/83-D. 11(B), dated January, 1984 
has referred the following dispute under Section 7-A and 
Section 10(1 )(d) of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 for 
adjudication by Ihis Tribunal. 

(2) The dispute is as follows : 

“Whether the demand of Tiiticorin Vattara General 
Workefs Union Tuticorin for absorption of S/Shri 
Sethuraman, R. Rosary, C. Ronald Fernando nhd 
T. Mohan, casual workers engaged in Exploratory 
Fisheries Project, Government of India, Tuticorin 
into regular service is justified 7 If so. to what 
relief the said four workmen are entitled ?" 

3. After Ihis Tribunal issued notice, the parties have app¬ 
eared nnd they fired claim statement and counter state¬ 
ment. 

4. The Union to substantiate in claim has lalsed the follow¬ 
ing grounds. The worker Sri Sethuraman was appointed 
from 5-4-1977 and he has put in more than 240 days of 
service. The worker Sri R. Rosary was appointed as Casual 
Deck Hand in January, 1978 and he has put in more than 
240 days of continuous service. The worker Sri C. Ronald 
Fernando had been working as Junior Deck Hand from 
19-12-1976 and he has been in continuous service since then 
and completed more than 240 days, The worker Thiru 
T. Mohan wns appointed an Casual Fitter from 16-3-1977 
and he too has completed more than 240 days of continuous 
service. As per Central Government’s Order in O.M. No. 
490 14-3-77 Est. (C) dated 30-7 1977, Ministry of Home 
Affairs (Department of Personnel and Administrative Re¬ 
forms), the Casual workers who have put in 240 dayy of 
continuous service within a period of two years should be 
absorbed in regular vacancies. The above mentioned workers 
have qualified themselves and have become entitled to be 
absorbed in the regular vacancies, blit they were not ap¬ 
pointed so. The Management ’ should appoint them as per¬ 
manent emnlovees under the provisions of the Tamilnadu 
Industrial Establishments (Conferment of Permanent Status 
to Workmen) Act, 1981. The. altitude of the Respondent- 
Management is incorrect and their failure to appoint thc 
Petitioners on permanent basis : s in violation of law. 

5. The claim of the Union was resisted bv the Respondent- 
Management stating that (he petition is not maintainable in 
law and on facts. It was not feasible for the Respondent to 
absorb Sri Sethuraman on reruFar baris. To keep him em¬ 
ployed, the Respondent offered him the post of Cook in one 
of lhe Vessels but hr declined to accept that offer, The 
claim of the Union that all (he petitioner' have become eligi¬ 
ble to be appointed on permanent basis is not correct. Thai 
Union is not entitled to anv rel'ef and the claim should be 
rejected. 




P«Tpr II— 3 ( ii)] 


4527 


VTTCJ TT tlargT - fer^n 23, lOS't/ffil 3, 1306 


(6)-By consent of both parties Eih. Ml* to M-19 on the 
side of the Management and W-l to W-4 on the vide of the, 
Union were marked- 

17) Thereafter, the learned counsel representing the Union 
and the learned counsel representing the’ Management made 
endorsement if the court records stating that the Peti¬ 
tioners 1 to 4 who were offered regular posts by the Res¬ 
pondent arc willing to accept the regular posts without any 
Condition. The learned counsel appearing for the Union in¬ 
dicated that in view of Ih eendorsenfent, no dispute survives 
for disposal or determination by this Tribunal. The endorse¬ 
ment will make it abundantly dear that the Respondent- 
Management had offered regular posts to the four workmen 
and the four have expressed their willingness to accept the 
regular posts without attaching any condition. Therefore the 
dispute raised by the Union have become solved. The 
Management which made the offer of regular posts to the 
Petitioners, which the Petitioners have agread to accept the 
same without anv condition should be taken in regular 
posts. 

(8) On that view, I hold that the dispute no loncer sur¬ 
vives and an award is passed In the above lines. There will 
be no order as to costs. 

Dated, this 17th dav of November, 1984. 

K. S. GURSMURTHY, Industrial Tribunal

2. As the reference- stands it involves the question il 
appointment of 74 workmen listed in the annexure. who 
were employed admittedly by the Cantonment Board from 
31-12-1976 and who, it is. an admitted fact, are continuously 
in the servied of the Cantonment Board from the said dale. 
The hitch which necessitated the present order of reference 
was the failure to conJinh them in the Board’s service. 
Now on behalf of the Union who is representing or espous¬ 
ing the cause of these workmen it is contended that since 
they have put in almost eight years of continuous service, 
these workmen belong nj to Class IV solving as Sweeper etc. 
deiervc to be confirmed, against which the contention of the 
Board is that since the recruitment of these 74 and 206 
other v.orkmen similarly placed was not made through an 
Employment Exchange, their appointment contravened Rule 
5-D(, 1) of the Cantonment Fund Servants Rules, 1937 which 
lays down that all first appointments for service under the 
Board shall be made through Employment Exchange or 
.such manner as the Central Government may d rect. It is 
therefore contended that since there was no direction from 
the Central Government not to recruit thtough Employment 
Exchange, the Board is unable to confirm these workmen in 
service and hence their reluctance. 

3. Undoubtedly Rule 5-B(l) above referred prescribes the 
recruitment through Employment Exchange and normally the- 
Board or their officers would not be in a position to make 
such appointments. Shri Ramakrishnan on behalf of the 
board informs that because of certain emergency the Board 
tilled in 250 posts belonging to Class-IV category and the 
concerned officers at the relevant time i.e. in tne year 1976 
ignoring Rule 5-B(l) of the Contonment Fund Servants Rules 
to meet the immediate need of the Board recruited these 
workmen and that they 'arc continuously working with the 
Board. He also tells us that the Board is not going to termi¬ 
nate anybody’s service. When the matter stands accordingly, 
when these employees belonging to the lowest strata have put 
in more than seven years of cont nuous service and when 
they joined the Board, they were in need of their i:e. woik- 
men’s service, now merely relying noon Rule 5-B(l) the 
Board cannot ignore their rights flowing from continuous 
service for more than seven years and they cannot ovoid 
confirming them, particularly when in the Boaid the normal 
period of confirmation is after six months’ service. If there . 
was any illegality committed, the illegality committed was 
by the Officer who appo'nted these workmen and not by 
the workmen and therefore for their regularities of the Offi¬ 
cer if there be any these workmen can never be allowed to 
suffer. ' Confirmation of service carries with it the security of 
,evice and having regard to the normal rules the continuous 
/ervice of seven years in my view invokes the said security. 

( therefore direct that in the light of peculiar circumstances 
discussed above these 74 workmen whose names are onne- 
xure to the shedule of reference should be confirmed by 
the Board in their service w ; thin one month from the date 
of publication of the Award. T am given to understand 
that ip all 280 similarly placed workmen are there 1 out of 
whom 'the reference covers 74 workmen. It is true that 
th^r case is not before me but s nee I am applymg a parti¬ 
cular rule ’ to these 74 workmen, the Board should aoply 
the 'time rule to the remaining 206 workmen be. similarly 
nlr.-cd workmen belonging to Class-lV, I am further given 
lo imde’stnnd that a list of 280 workmen is already submit- 
te t to the nuhorries and therefore there is no difficulty in 
identifying the 280 workmen including the 74 workmen 
referred to in the order of reference. 

4. The present instance however shall not be quoted as 
a precedent for the purpose of any appointment to be made 
bv fh* Board in theV service and the rules bv which the 
servi'^e is governed shall be scrupulously followed. The 
present instance should be treated hs an exception in the 
exceptional circumstances. Award acordhigly. 

, M. A. DESHPANDE, Presiding Officer

.AWARD 

1 The dispute in this reference under Section 10(1) (d) of 
the Industrial Disputes Act is between civilian employees 
workjng under the Commandant, Embarkation Headquarters 
and the Embarkation Headquarters. The reference is worded 
ns follows :— 

“Whether the workmen of the Embarkation Head¬ 
quarters, Bombay who perform same and similar 
duties as dock workers are entitled to usages and 
service conditions on par with other dock w-orkers 
employed by the concerned employers in Bombay ? 
Tf so, to what relief arc the concerned workmen 
entitled". 

The Transport and Dock Workers Union representing these 
civilian employees filed a statement of claim stating therein 
that the Embarkation Commandant cleans and forwards ex¬ 
port and Import cargo at the Bombay Port. It essentially fun¬ 
ctions as a ‘clearing and forwarding agent* in every sense of 
the term. These civilian employees arc employed for this 
work but are paid such scales of pay as are applicable to 
other government servants. They are not even taken into 
account by any Pay Commission and tlxrir salaries are fixed 
arbitrarily without any normal standard. They perform the 
same duty as the workmen of clearing and forwarding agents 
at Bombay Docks. Employees of clearing and forwarding ag¬ 
ents receive much higher wage and enjoy much better sendee 
conditions than these employees. There is also according to 
them no machinery available to them to ventilate their Gri¬ 
evances and their salaries and service conditions are not con¬ 
sidered by the Pay Commission. As a result, their payscalcs 
are stagnant and fiixed arbitrarily.. 

2. The Union then contended that it raised a dispute with 
the Regional Labour Commissioner and contended that these 
civilian employees of the Embarkation Commandant who 
perform duties on par with and similar to the cmplovees 
of the clearing and frownrding agents and other dock workers 
as defined bv the Dock Workers (Regulation of 'Employ¬ 
ment) Act, 1947 should he paid on par wadi Mori, workers’ 
at Bombay”. Raising of this dispute resulted ultimately in 
this reference. 

3. Employees of the Embarkation Headquarters, accord¬ 
ing to the Union, undertake the responsibility of 'clear in a and 
forwarding of export and import cargo entrusted to them 
and also import and export cargo eotniHed to them. Tt fur¬ 
ther said that it works as a registered clearing and forward¬ 
ing agent, though it is said that the union does not know 
whether it is actually registered. The work, consists of import¬ 
ing and exporting of cargo, receipt, discharge, clearing and 
forwarding of cargo, loading and uo’ondine. stacking and 
destaek’ng, packmn and re-packing of cargo in and around 
the vicinity of the Bombay Port Trust, and that ihe-e work¬ 
men arc covered bv the definition of ‘dock workers* under 
the dock workers’ (ReeulatL n of Employment! Act. Ttwy, 
therefore, claim that the schemes mode thereunto-- bv the 
Wane Rnard for the Port and Dock Workers should be made 
applicable to them. It then s#t out the definition of the wo-d 
"dock workers” in the Act and therefore demanded that since 


they are doing the same kind of work they should be paid 
the same wage as dock wo,kers get. The claim, thcrefoie, 
it that they arc not only dock workers as defined under the 
Act but arc also entitled to the various schemes made there¬ 
under between the dock workers and the Government of 
]nd>a. The union also stated in para 11 of the statement of 
claim that they are covered by the agreement between the 
representatives of dock workers at Bombay and employers’ 
organisations such as Custom House and Clearing and For¬ 
warding Agents’ Association, Bombay. At the same time it 
also submitted that the seivice conditions of the workmen 
arc governed by the schemes of the Wage Board for Port & 
Dock Workers in major Ports in India. 

4. Thih Commandant, Embarkation Headquarters filed his 
reply or statement disputing the averments made by the emp¬ 
loyees. According to him the Embarkation Headquarters is a 
department of the Ministry of Defence and employees civi 
lian staff also for Its work over and above defence personnel. 
The ratio of defence personnel to civilian employees is 
43 : 57. The Embarkation Headquarters amongst otners is 
responsible for handling and clearing of defence cargo by air 
and sea from and to stations abroad. There are other func¬ 
tions with which we are not concerned in this reference.and 
need not be referred also. But the Commandant points out 
that tlxe handling of cargo by sea and matters connected 
with it is one of the derence functions and that the Head¬ 
quarters ‘handles and clears cargo for the , Ministry of 
Defence only* and therefore it does work which Is termed as 
"Self Clearance". 

5. For this, it Is admitted, that some civilian employees 
like clerks, tally clerks, labourers, labours muktvdams are. 
engaged for the import and export procedure but It is wrong 
to say that they have a licence from the Custom Authorities 
to act as agents for clearing of Import and export of goods 
belonging to other parties. According to it, therefore, it does 
not come, within the category of clearing and forwarding 
agents. Under the Customs Act, it is pointed out that persons 
doing clearance of goods can do "self clearance’ and need not 
have a licence, Since they are doing self clearance they are 
not required to register with the Bombay Port Trust. 

6, With regard to the service conditions, it fa pointed out 
tl.<at the clerical staff is transferable within the actions and 
also transferable anywhere in India. These other sections of 
the organisaljon have nothing to do with the handling of 
cargo or any work connected with the port and may also 
work away from the porta. These workmen have also been 
transferred from the dock areas to far of places, sometimes 
on t(iclr own request and sometimes on account of the 
department’s directions. 

7. Functioning in connection wilh the cargo of the head¬ 
quarters in Bombay is then pointed out by the Commandant. 
It says that the cargo on the ship (ill It is landed on the 
jetty is the responsibility of the stevedores. Tallying the 
cargo which is landed and its further movement from the 
jet'y to the sheds is the responsibility of the Bombay Port 
Trust Employees. The tallv clerics of the Embarkation Head 
quarters merely keep a tallv of cargo landed from chartered 
vessel"* for Its own security and accounting purposes. The 
tallies prepared by the employees of the Embarkation Head- 
ouarters have no official recognition wilh the Bombay iPort 
Trust. The Embarkation Hetiklouarters employees are not 
allowed to work on board the shin nor can they be allowed 
to shift the cargo to the sheds. The labourers at the Embar¬ 
kation Headquarters encaged at the port are purely for tak¬ 
ing over canro from ihe Port Trust’s Shed Superintendent 
and for loading it into .vehicles for moving them outside the 
Docks. The work which thev do, therefore, is in no way 
connected with the loading of ships or movement of vessels. 
The work according to the Embarkation Headquarters is like, 
of a person clearing and forwarding his own coods imported 
through a ship. Such workers, acccrrtimj to the Embarkation 
Headauarter.s cannot be equated with "Dock Workers”. 
Merely because they are associated incidently with the work 
at the Port Trust It does not follow thal they become Dock 
Workers. 

8. Tt then pointed out that civilian employees are Gov¬ 
erned bv the Civil Service Regulations nppliacble and their 
categories have been considered by three Pay Commissions

and that tore is also a machinery for the ventilation of 
grievances of the employees. They are governed by The 
Science Service (Revised Pay) Roles which are framed on 
the lines of the recommendations of the Pay Commission. 

9. The employer then de’nied that the employees perform 
the same duties as workmen doing clearing and forwarding 
of cargo in the Bombay Docks. Bombay Docks is one of tho 
working areas of the Embarkation Headquarters and there 
are other areas away from the Port area also. In substance, 
therefore, the contention is that 1'h.e employees are not com; 
any work connected with “clearing and forwarding of 
cargo, lading and unloading of cargo in the sense in which 
the clearing and forwarding agents do’’. Being consignee of 
the goods the Embarkation. Headquarters has to arrange for 
felf clearance of the goods as, any other person doing the 
clearance of his goods. Mere work in the vicinity of the 
dock docs not confer the status of “dock workers” on these 
civilian employees. They are Central Government employees 
and do not become Dock Workers amenable to discipline 
of the docks. They cannot, therefore be Dock workers with¬ 
in the meaning of Dock-Workers’ Act. The agreement entered 
into between the representatives of the Dock workers and 
the Clearing and Forwarding Agents also are not applicable 
to them. The clearing and forwading of goods is done by the 
Embarkation Headquarters as owner of the goods. 


10- The contention is also raised that the activities do 
not constitute an ‘industry’ within Section 2(j) of the In¬ 
dustrial Disputes Act, 1947 as it clears goods imported for 
the use of the Army, Navy and the Air Force of the country 
and is connected with thte sovereign functions of the State. 

It also raised the contention that the Central Government 
is not the appropriate Government for the purposes of 
forming an opinion under Section 2(a) (i) of the Act and 
the Tribunal therefore) ht not competent to entertain thi^ 
reference. 

IE- A rejoinder was filed to this written statement and 
among other things by way of deniel it .was pointed out 
that the Embarkation Headquarters also clears cargo of pri¬ 
vate organizations Iiko Bharat Dynamics Ltd., Praga Tools 
Lt, and many other organizations. That it is on industry and 
not only it handles its own cargo but also cargo of other 
organizations in the dock. On the basis of equal pav for 
equal work, since, civilian workers to the same kind of work 
and perform same and similar duties as work of clearing ana 
forwarding agents they claim to be Dock Workers and ihe 
same pay, The Embarkation Headquarters when it functions 
as a cargo handling agent, It is an industry within the mean¬ 
ing of Section 2(j) of the Act and the Central Government 
i$ the appropriate Government in making the reference. 

12. Before proceeding further, it would be advisable to ad¬ 
vert to the terms of the reference again. It appears thru there 
is considerable confusion between the parties to this refe¬ 
rence as to the scope and as to how it was referred for 
adjudication and what evidence was necessary to enable ad¬ 
judication on the referred subject In dispute. For the Embar¬ 
kation Headquarters, it was urged that what the civilian emp 
lovees are seeking is that they should he classified as Dock 
Workers to whom (l*e Dock Workers' Regulation Act applies. 
It also contended that whether the work which is done by 
the civilian employees of the. Embarkation Headquarters is 
“similar” or same like Dock Workers is required to be ad¬ 
judicated in this reference. It was ai-xt pointed Out and ad¬ 
mittedly that.the reference does not express in clear terms 
ns to whether the various schemes which are prevailing fdr 
Dock Workers arid the control of Dock Workers as coo 
temnlated under the Dock Workers’ Act would govern the 
workmen so as to become Dock Workers under the Dock 
Workers’ Act and pass from the control of the Embarkation 
Headquarters to the dock Board under the Dock Workers 
Act. Mr. Narurkar for the Embarkation Headquarters 

pf one stage wnnfed to point out what arc the various 
schemes under the Dock Workers’ Act for dock workers 
and how . they function. He wanted to strenuously 

contended that the Tribunal aught adjudicate on the ques¬ 
tion whether rhe civilian emnlovees are performing "same 
or similar duties as Dock Workers". Not much attention and 
practically no attention was paid to the other Important 


question referred to the Tribunal for adjudication as to 
whether the workmen are entitled to wages and service con 
ditions on par with Dork Workers employed by the concern¬ 
ed employers in Bombay” and if so to what relief the con¬ 
cerned workmen 'are entitled. I shall point out how this has 
led to the result which has followed in this reference. 

13. If the Embarkation Headquarters was not clear as U> 
what was required to be done by it and what, was referred 
to the Tribunal for adjudication the union appears to be also 
contused. It was uppaicntly iu two mindy whether the civi¬ 
lian employees vvero entitled to be treated as Dock work¬

between the Customs Clearing ant Forwarding Agents’ Asso¬

referred In Its statement that the work of the Embarkation 
Headquarters is essentially like that of Clearing and For¬ 
warding Agents and at other places to the agreements bet¬ 
ween such owners and organization and the Dock Workers’ 
Union will govern the service conditions of these employees, 
at other places and in the evidence placed emphasis upon the 
definition of Dock Workers under the Act and what was 
paid to the Dock Workers under the Wage Board for Port 
' and Dock Workers in the major ports under the award. Except 
by producing their list of* duties and the salary scales which

14. Tuning once again to t~_- terms of reference. It will 
be clear that the subject referred for adjudication falls into 
three parts. First, identification of workmen of the Embar¬ 
kation Headquarters who perform same and similar duties as 
Dock Workers; second, whether they are entitled to the same 
/‘wages and service conditions on par with other Dock Wor¬ 
kers employed by tho concerned employers in Bombay.” 

15. In view of the phraseology employed, it would be 
difficult to hold that rise question as to whether the work 
which the employees of the Embarkation are doing in con¬ 
nection wilh the loading and unloading of cargo at the Bom¬ 
bay Port is “same and similar” to the work of other dock 
workers is a question referred for adjudication. On q plain 
reading of the terms of the reference, it seems to be quite 
clear that the duties performed by the Embarkation Head¬ 
quarters by the civilian employees nre to be taken as they 
arc. Where similar duties are performed by dock workers 
the next question which is required to be decided is whether 
thev are entitled to wages and service conditions on par 
with such dock workers “emplovcd by concerned employers 
in Bombay” find if not what relief can be given to them. It 
is quite clear that the drafting of the reference leaves much 
to be desired. But I am quite dear in my mind that no ad B 
indication is called for on the question as to whether the 
duties performed by the civilian employees of the Embar¬ 
kation Headquarters qualify for being described ns Dock- 
Workers. If that was the intention of the reference, then the 
reference would have been worded as to whether the Embar¬ 
kation Headquarters emnlovees arc dock workers within the 
meaning of the Act and if so what should be their wages 
and services conditions” or whether they lire entitled to same 
or similar wages as dock workers of the Bombay Port Trust. 

Ifi. Elerc again the reference speaks of wattes and service 
conditions of other Dock workers but not engaged particularly 
hv the Bombay Port Trust coming within the purview of 
the Dock Labour Board but “bv the concerned employers 
in Bombay". This to my mind dearly suggests that the word 

Dock woikers" used in the reference is of a larger applica¬ 
tion titan Dock workers coming within the purv : ew of Ihe 
DoeV T obOtir Ronrd Regulation Act and covered by the 
Do>-k Labour Board Schemer. It minis to the winder defi- 
n'tion under (he Act and also indica'es all those who are-

loarfme end un-ioading of cargo, and includes also v/orkmcn

rimilnr to such employees of the Clearing nnd Forwarding

agents in the statement of claim of the Dock Workers’ Union 
clearly indicates what was m mind of the Government and 
what was the case put forward during conciliation, It appeals 
that the confusion has set in only later. With this initial 
difficulty got over and clarified it scents to me that what the 
Tribunal ts required, by the terms of the reference is to

The next task is to find out what are their wages and service

therefore, and on the terms of the reference, though it has

fif Who are the workmen of the Embarkation Head¬ 
quarters who perform same and similar duties as 
Dock woikcrs employed by concerned employers 
in Bombay;

The categories of employees engaged by the Embarkation 
Headquarters is produced at Exhibit ‘A’ along with the docu¬

19. The Dock Workers Regulation Act of 1948 defines who 
-3 11 Dock worker and also lays down tvhnt a scheme for 
Dock workers has to nrovide, A ’dock worker’ means ‘a 
person employed or to be onlploved in nr in the vicinity of 
n nort for work in connection with loadmg or unloading of 
cargo". We may not refer lo the other parts of the Act as

admittedly the emtio) ecs are not concerned with the prepa¬ 
ration ot ships 101 die receipt or discharge ol cargo or 
leaving port, Ihe question, howuicr, raised is whether tnesc

workmen aie wort ng in the vicinity of the Pori they cannot

out cither for storage or transfer by tliij Embarkation Head¬

Headquarters sheds and office are also situated admittedly

None of these contentions are really of use and even if lltey 
are admitted and accepted in this case, the employees are 
connected and concerned with the loading and unloading of 
cargo and its movement. The goods removed from the ship 
do not cease to be cargo merely because of their landing 
on the jetty. They continue to be cargo until the consignee 
takes possession thereof. If the person happens to be an 
agent of the consignee, then the agent would be all the 
more handling the cargo of the consignee and engaged in 
loading and unloading of consignment and storage of such 
cargo. The circumstances at this stage-were clarified on behalf 
of the Embarkation Headquarters and considerable argument 
was raised at one stage that wb.vt the Embarkntiona Head¬ 
quarters was engaged in is to receive and clear its own cargo. 
It is now admitted that it not merely handles cargo of De¬ 
fence Department, but aho for various other public sector 
concerns such as Pm cm Tools, Mazgaon Dock, Hindustan 
Aeronautics, Border Roads Development Board, etc, a list 
of which cart be found at appendix ‘B’ to the list of docu¬ 
ments filed by the Union on 21-6-1984, This position is not 
disputed and it is also conceded that the Embarkation Head¬ 
quarters charges Rs. ?00[- per tonne as Handling charges to 
the consignees for their cargo. This disposes of also the 
coniention of the Embarkation Headquarters that it is noi 
an ’industry’ and that it is only engaged in self clearance. 
It is cicely a service industry and also it is not engaged in 
self clearance alone but also acts as clearing and forwarding 
agent. The circumstance that it is not so registered with the 
authorities is irrelevant and if no consequence to this .case. 

20. It will be seen from the above discussion that the 
employees of the Embarkation Headquarters do similar and 
same work or less akin to the work of the. Dock workers 
engaged in clearing and forwarding. Tt is an admitted posi¬ 
tion that unloading of cargo, rs I have held above, v.-hen it 
is transferred form the jetty to the SPT si teds is a task. 
Undertaken by the Port Trust employees and shin’s crew, 
Therefore, these workmen perform the same and similar 
duties like civilian employees who are encaged bv forwarding 
nnd clearing agents. Unfortunately, neither the Embarkation 
Headquarters nor the Union produced, before me any evi¬ 
dence to show what are the service, condtions and wages of 
such employees, and I am told. Mid I had occasion to see 
the' agreements which govern the wages scales and service 
conditions of snch etnnlovces between the employer.-,’ associ¬ 
ation and the Dock Workers’ Union. For reasons not known 
and it is difficult to envisage ihe Union did not produce anv 
settlement, TliE is particularly curious in ’’leiv of trie refe 
renoe to the clearing and forwarding ngcnls’ owners asso¬ 
ciations and the settlement iff the Dock workers’ Union in 
their statement of el rim. T have, therefore, nothin" before 
me from which to know what are the service conditions of 
such workmen for clearing and forwarding agents, xvbelher 




4532 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : DECEMBER 29, 1984/PAUSA 8, 1906 [Part II—Sfc. 3(ii)] 


they arc different or same, and if they are not the same or 
similar what should be the relief given to the civilian em¬ 
ployees of the Embarkation Headquarters. Though, theic- 
fore, the employees of the Embarkation Headquarters are 
Dock workers within the meaning of the Act being engaged 
in the vicinity of the Port in connection with loading and 
unloading and movement and storage of cargo like workmen 
of clearing and Forwarding agents, I have no materia! before 
me from which I can say whether the conditions of em¬ 
ployment of civilian employees are same or similar, or 
better or worse, and wbnt relief should be given to then], 
'Hint there does appear a difference in their service con¬ 
ditions than those of the civilian employees is apparent on 
the evidence of Capt, Varma of the Embarkation Headquar¬ 
ters. But the parties have not chosen to give to the Tribunal 
the necessary material, Tie result, therefore, is that the 
civilian employees of the Embarkation Headquparters in the 
above categories must he held to he doing the same and 
similar kind of work as (be employees of the clearing and 
forwarding agents engaged in the Bombay Fort. I cannot 
grant them any relief nor make any declaration as to whe¬ 
ther they are entitled to same or similar service conditions. 
NormaP.v same kind of work will attract same kind of 
wages. Tbrt is onlv what I can sav at this stage. In the 
absence of any evidence, it cannot be said that their con¬ 
ditions of service and wages are similar. Hancc no relief 
can bg granted to them. 

a* 

21. Award accordingly. 

R. D. TULPUl.E, Presiding Officer 
(No. L-1301(6)/83-D. 11(B)] 
HART SINGH, Desk Officer

S.O. 4723.—Whereas the Ccntrul Government huving been 
satisfied that the public interest so required had, in pursuance 
of the provisions of sub-clause (vi) of clause (n) of section 2 
of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), 

declared by the notification of the Government of India in 
the Ministry of Labour and Rehabilitation, Department of 
Labour S O. 1861 dated the 25th May. 1984, the Bank Note 
Press, Dess as (MP) to be a public utility servieu for the 
purposes of the said Act, for a period of six month, from 
the 15th July, 1984 ; 


And whereas, the Central Government is of opinion that 
public interest requires the extension of the said period by 
a further period of six months ; 

Nc>w, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by the 
proviso to sub-clause (vi) of clause (n) of section 2 of the 
Industrial Disputes Act. 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central 
Government hereby declares the said industry to he a public 
utility service for the purpose, of the said Act, for a further 
period of six months from the. 15th lanuary, 1985.

New Delhi, the 11th December, 1984 

S.O. 4725,—Whereas Messrs Rajasthan State Cooperative 
Bank Limited, Nehru Bazar, Jaipur, (RJ/816) (hereinafter 
referred to as the said establishment! have applied for ex¬ 
emption under sub-section (2A) of Section 17 of the Em¬ 
ployees’ Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 
1952 (19 of 1952) (hereinafter referred to as the said Act)) 

And whereas, the Central Government is satisfied that 
the employees of the said establishment arc, without mak¬ 
ing any separate contribution or payment of premium, in 
enjoyment of benefits under the Group Insurance Scheme 
of the Life Insurance Corporation of Tndia in the nature of 
Life Insurance which arc tnore favourable to such em¬ 
ployees than the benefits admissible under the Employees’ 
Deposit Linked Insurance Scheme, 1976 (hereinafter refer¬ 
red to ns the said Scheme): 



45-34 


THE GAZETTE CF INDIA : DECEMBER-29, 19S4/MUSA 8, 1906 


[Part II Sec. 3(ii)] 


Now, therefore, in exercise of the poweis conferred by 
sub-section (2A) of section 17 of the said Act and subject 
to Uas conditions specified in the Schedule annexed hereto, 
the Central Government hereby ‘exempts the said establish¬ 
ment lrom the operation of all the prov.sions of 1 the said 
Scheme for a period of three, years. 

SCHEDULE 

1. 'The employer m idation to the said establishment shall 
submit such returns to the Regional Provident Fund Com¬ 
missioner, Rajasthan and maintain such accounts and pro¬ 
vide such facilities for inspection, as the Central Govern- 
muy direct from time to tune. 

2. The employer shall pay such inspection charges as 
the Central Government may, ftom time to time, ditect 
under clause (it) of sub-scciion (3A) of section 17 of the 
said Act, within 15 days from the close of every month. 

3. Ail expenses involved in the administration of tne 
Group Insurance Scheme, including maintenance of accounts, 
Mibmtssion of returns, payment of 'insurance premia, trans¬ 
fer of accounts, payment of inspection charges etc, shall he 
borne by the employer. 

4. The employer shall display on the Notice Eoard of 

the establishment, a copy of the rules of the Group Insur¬ 
ance Scheme ns approved by the Central Government and 
as and when amended, nlongwith a translation of the salient 
features thereof, in the language of the majority of the 
employee*., , 

5. Whereas an employee, who is already a member of the 
Employees’ Provident Fund or the Provident Fund of an 
establishment exempted under the said Act. is employed In 
bin establishment, the employer shall immediately enrol him 
as a member of the Group Insurance Scheme end pay neces¬ 
sary premium in respect of him to the Life Insurance Cor¬ 
poration of India. 

6 . The employer shall arrange tp enhance the benefits 
available to the employees under the Group Insurance 
Scheme appropriately, if the benefits available to the em¬ 
ployees under the said Scheme are enhanced, so that the 
benefits available under the Group Insurance Scheme pre 
more favourable to the employees than the benefits admissi¬ 
ble under the said Scheme. 

7. Notwithstanding anything contained in the Group 
Insurance Schemo if on the death of an employee the amount 
payable under this scheme be less than the amount that 
would be payable had employee been covered under the said 
Scheme, the employer shall pay the difference to the legal 
he:,-/nominee of the employee, as compensation. 

,S. No amendment of the provisions of the Group Insur¬ 
ance Scheme, shall be made without the prior approval of 
tile Regonal Provident Fund Commissioner, Rajasthan and 
where any amendment is likely to affect adversely the in¬ 
terest of the employees, the Regional provident Fund Com¬ 
missioner shall before giving his approval, give a reasonable 
epportunity to the employees to explain their point of 
vievy. 

9. Where, for any reason, the employees of the sSId estab¬ 
lishment do not remain covered under the Group Insurance 
Scheme of the Life Insuranse Corporation of India as already 
adopted by tho said establishment, or the benefits to the 
employees under this Scheme are reduced in any manner, 
the exemption shall be liable ‘‘to be cancelled. 

10. Where, for any reason, the employer falls to pay the 
premium etc. within the due date, as fixed by the Life 
Insurance Corporation of India, and the policy is allowed to 
lapse, the exemption is liable to be cancelled, 

11. In case of default, if any made by the employer in 
paymeht of premium and responsibility for payment of 
assurance benefits to the nominees or the legal heirs of 
deceased members who would have been covered under the 
said Scheme but for grant of this exemption, shall be that 
of the employer. 

12. Upon the death of the members covered under the 
Scheme the Life Insurance Corporation of India shall ensure 
prompt pavment of the sum assured to the nominees/Legal 
heirs of the. deceased member entitled for it and In snv 
case with'n one month from the receipt of claim complete 
in all respects". .

Life Insurance which are more favourable to such tm-

Group Insurance Scheme, including maintenance of accounts, 
submission of returns, payment of insurance premia, trans-

Scheme appropriately, if the benefits available to the em¬ 
ployees under the su'd Scheme are enhanced, so that the 
benefits available under the Group Insurance Scheme are 
move favourable to the employees than the benefits admissi¬ 
ble under the said ‘Scheme.
